ParlaMint-DK_20190409130014	I give the floor to the Prime Minister for an introductory statement. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409130019	Thank you very much. After all, the electoral period is nearing its end, which means that we have been in work for four years now. When we arrived, we took immediate action in foreign policy: tighter requirements for family reunification, consistency with rejected asylum seekers, lower benefits for new arrivals. Shortly thereafter, Europe was seriously opened up, people on the run rushed unhindered across the external borders, continued up through the countries, including Denmark, and the task we took on in that situation almost four years ago was to lead our country through the migration and refugee crisis in a proper manner. One can think about what would have happened if we had not had a change of government before then, because we took over after a government that relaxed and relaxed the immigration policy, unlike my government, which has pursued a strict immigration policy. We have done this with the awareness that the fate of each refugee is unhappy; that Denmark has a responsibility to help people flee; but that we also have a responsibility for our own country, to look after Denmark. We've kept our heads cold and our hearts warm. Because we can't let in more than we can hold. We have to take care of Denmark, and the number matters. The last two years are the number of asylum seekers for Denmark at the lowest level for 10 years. This is, of course, due to the efforts of the European Union: fewer people are coming in, among others. because Denmark's line has gained more and more support. But this is also due to the policy we have pursued in this country, where we have tightened, reduced benefits, met people with consequences, we have made adjustments together with the Danish People's Party, and which have produced clear results. Denmark has gone from being no. 5 in the EU, measured by the number of asylum seekers per year. resident, to be no. 17, measured by the number of asylum seekers per year. citizen. This is the change that has taken place and that has nothing to do with how many refugees are coming to Europe, but has something to do with how the refugees get to Europe: where are they going? Therefore, it is relevant to ask ourselves: What had happened, and where would we be today, if we had not made the tightenings that have reduced our attraction if one can express it that way? If Denmark had continued to be in fifth place today, yes, in the last four years we would have received 85,000 asylum seekers, measured up to the 35,000 we actually received, that is. 50,000 extras over 50,000 extras. And then I didn't count the family reunions. With 50,000 more, we would have to spend more tax dollars on asylum seekers and refugees, have less money for welfare, and we would have been able to make much less effort in the neighbourhood: we would have had to cut the effort. The situation out there in the Middle East and in Africa is still unstable. The pressure on our neighbouring countries remains great, you can just see over the sound to Sweden, which in the first 2 months of the year, i.e. the first 2 months of this year, has accepted as many asylum seekers as Denmark had last year. That is why I am concerned here, where the season goes on, that the tight policy that the government has built up step by step risks being torn down again, stump by piece, if there is another majority after the next elections, because that is what will happen, and we all know that. We all know that: there will be relaxation again. And why would that be? It will, of course, be because the whole parliamentary basis for a possible new Prime Minister calls for relaxation. But it is also because the Social Democrats themselves want to see a break with the low benefits which are the cornerstone of a rigid immigration policy: a break with the ceiling on cash aid, a break with the benefits of integration. And it will create three problems at once: it will make Denmark • again more attractive as an asylum country. This will create less incentive to move away from unemployment into employment. This will mean spending more money on support and less money on welfare. What a divided government with a divided parliamentary basis can cause in the field of foreigners, the past shows. So if the majority changes after elections, I have to say that I am deeply concerned about the future, and that is why it is so crucial that we stick to the line that has been laid and that has shown its effect and that we can today also measure the value of, among other things. the value that Denmark gives a historic hand to the surrounding areas; that Denmark is the country after Germany and Italy which, in total terms, provides the most to build up the EU trust fund for Africa, a trust fund that has meant quite concretely that 42,000 people on their way up into Libya, across the Mediterranean, have now been sent back where they came from.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409142643	We're opening the debate, and that's sir. Jesper Petersen, Social Democratic Party. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409142716	Thank you. After all, there has been a long discussion about the future in this area of the tax complaint structure. Before Christmas, however, all parties in the Folketing could reach an agreement to change the complaint structure and address the problem of over-long processing times experienced by citizens and companies for a long time now when they have had complaints for tax purposes. On average 23 months, citizens and businesses have had to wait for a case to be settled and dealt with, and that, of course, is no good. It is essential for confidence in our tax administration that you as a citizen and as a company see that your case is dealt with properly, effectively and professionally, and that legal certainty is at its top, and that therefore also means that there is a reasonable period of time, a reasonable period of time, and it is too long today. In addition to measures to bring down the processing times, the agreement also offers changes to the Board of Appeal structure. It has been in this way over a period of time that in some places they had their own assessment boards and tax appeal boards, and in some places where they had therefore mixed the tasks together in the Joint Appeals Board. And with the changes in the area of property valuation and an evaluation of the state of affairs in the complaint structure, we have now agreed to move to an assessment board and tax appeal board. As I said, this has been a rather long process. Back in May 2018, the Minister of Taxation set out what we thought was a centralisation, with only ten tax courts and significantly fewer members than today, who are thus involved in being laymen and involved in making decisions in the field of taxation when there are complaints. And we are pleased with the cooperation we have had between the parties. We took the time to get a good process and also came to a place where we could see that it had ended much better than what was planned, and therefore now 22 tax courts, 176 members and ten specially appointed. It is a little new that there should then be specially appointed, who are not only laymen, but also have some sort of extra insight. This is also one of the things to be included in the evaluation that has been made, but which we agree to, so that we try to have the ten specially appointed members in addition to those who are laymen and appointed, simply because they want to make the effort that it is to help to have a good tax review structure and assess these cases, and therefore also 15 rating boards with a total of 350 members. The agreement also makes some changes to the division of tasks. Several cases will be settled in the tax courts and the free choice system will be abolished, so that the more fundamental cases will still remain in the national tax law, but therefore more cases than are currently decided by the tax courts. The agreement also has some wordings which emphasize the role of the Tax Appeals Board as the secretariat for the mentionrs, and behind the way in which it is formulated there is in any case for us to see the view that the decision should not be taken on the board, but that it is primarily the secretariat and gives their input to the boards which have to take the proper decisions. However, more funds are being provided, not least at the Tax Appeals Board, where the cork has been in relation to reducing the processing time. There are a number of different consultation responses. After all, the Lawyers' Council and Danish Occupation point out that they are not entirely satisfied and that they do not believe that reducing the duration of the proceedings should be remedied by moving cases from the National Tax Court. You are quite right to point out that it is the Tax Appeals Board which is primarily a bottleneck for the cases. As I have said, we are very aware of this, and that is why it is also part of the agreement that we are providing more resources to the Tax Appeals Board and that actions are being taken to improve the effectiveness of the complaints process. There are also a number of persons entitled to a hearing who are concerned that the courts will be able to keep up if there is an increase in the number of cases due to direct judicial review, which will become possible more quickly than today. The Minister has assured us that the current possibility of judicial review has not led to a significant increase in the number of cases and gives an assurance that what is planned can be done. All in all, I would say that we are, of course, helping to implement the agreement we have reached and we think that we have ended up in a good place, which is much better than where the process started. There are still some issues that we would like to clarify, and also some issues that I am getting in from some of the people who are active in these appeal boards that we will then bring forward. All in all, however, we believe that a long process has ended with a good complaint structure, where laymen still play a major role and where hopefully we can do something strong and active to reduce the disproportionate length of time available in this area.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409143221	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments, so we move on to Mr. Dennis Flydtkjær, Danish People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409143231	After all, we are discussing L 212 on a new Board of Appeal structure. This is not a matter that has had much media attention, and it is also an area that I think most people will hopefully not need. After all, it is a matter of the fact that when you have disagreed with the tax authorities, you must of course have a good opportunity to complain and have your case tested. However, for those people who then need to be complained, it is important that there is good legal certainty and that you get the best possible assessment of your case. There we have made an agreement from December 2018, in which all parties are involved, and that is why I can already say that the Danish People's Party supports the bill. The agreement deals, inter alia, with: on a new Board of Appeal structure, on Circulation, on the number of members of the Board of Appeal and on the complaints dealt with in the Boards of Appeal and the National Tax Court respectively. And as the previous rapporteur also mentioned, the aim is that there has been a very long time in which to deal with the matter, and that is what we have tried to do. It also has, of course, a legal perspective, i.e. that you have had to wait a very long time to get a decision on your tax assessment. The agreement is, of course, a compromise, and I think we all have it that way. Something is good and something is less good, but I think that overall it is a good compromise that has been reached. Normally you brag about what you have got into an agreement, but I would really like to say that I am actually really glad that there are some things we have taken out of the proposal that the government made from the beginning. Because there were a great many things in the original proposal that were actually a deterioration in legal certainty from my point of view, that is, where you went in and looked at how you could reduce the citizens' ability to complain. One example of this is that you, for example, have been able to do something about it. should not be allowed to attend and meet with the caseworker. We think this was a wrong approach, because the report that came out, that is to say the evaluation of the existing system, showed quite clearly, I think it was in the Tax Appeals Board that there was a challenge. So we did not think it was right that the rights of the citizen should be impaired. So I would actually just like to say that I think it is very good that we got something out of the draft that was originally on the negotiating table. The specific content of it includes a number of different things, but among other things. Some types of cases were moved between the tax courts and the National Tax Court. Some types of cases move down and others move up. And we think there's good sense in that, that is, the things we've found there. Then there is the opt-out system that will be abolished. In other words, before, there was freedom of choice for citizens to choose between a tax court or the National Tax Court. There has been some criticism of this in the hearings, and we do not think this is particularly good as a starting point. But it is, of course, a question of making better use of our tax appeal board, which, of course, did not receive much attention. And I think it is cancelled because many thought that they had a higher professional judgement of their case in the National Tax Court. in this agreement and also with the bill set some things in motion to strengthen the Appeals Boards. Among other things: There are ten professionals with tax insight into the Boards of Appeal. Some integration courses are being developed to increase professionalism from the start, and this is something that needs to be done with the tax unions. So in this way we will also strengthen the tax courts, what I believe and hope can make citizens feel good and fair treated there too. Then there are the initiatives to go straight to the courts so that you do not have to wait six months, and that is also an excellent initiative. So all in all, we think it is a fine compromise that we have reached widely among the parties of the Folketing, so that is why we can support the bill from my party.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409143616	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments, so we move on to Mrs Louise Schack Elholm, Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409143625	Thank you. After all, we were faced with a situation in which we could see that the complaint times in the complaint system were far too long, and that is actually something we have been working on all the years we have had the power of government: in various ways to reduce the processing time. And one of the places where we could also work to do that was here with the board structure. Another challenge we had was that there was also criticism of the diversity and professionalism of some of the decisions of the Boards of Appeal. These were some of the things we looked into when we looked at what we should do in relation to the complaint structure. And that is why I am very pleased with the agreement that we have made with the Social Democrats, the Danish People's Party, the Enhedslisten, the Alternative, the Radical Left and the SF, to make both some tax appeal boards and some assessment boards, so that there is a greater focus on the narrow academic areas. Because it's not the same fields you have to concentrate on. We have held onto the layman's board because we think it is important that ordinary people are also included in these boards, but at the same time we have got some in with a strong professionalism in the specific areas. And we hope that will help to ensure that both this strengthened professionalism and a strong layman kick in what ensures that we also have a sense of what is happening locally in the various places. There is no doubt that it has also been difficult for us to abolish the free choice model, in relation to whether you would have your cases in the tax courts or in the National Tax Court. But we just have to admit that it didn't work very well. It helped delay matters. We had already to give it up in relation to the 3F cases that completely stopped the National Tax Court. Now we have taken the consequence and decidedly abolished it. ParlaMint-DK_20190409143845	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments, and then it is Mr. Rune Lund, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409143906	There's a good political agreement behind this bill. It is a political agreement which means that we strengthen legal certainty, the processing times, and that we strengthen the layman element in our tax review system. So the political agreement, and hence this bill, will ensure both that legal certainty is high and that processing times are down. It is an agreement which also means that 200 million will be added in the next few years. kr. to the complaint system. It wasn't certain it was gonna end here. When we had the first meeting on a hot day last year before the summer holidays, the tax minister came with ice, but the good taste of ice was inversely proportional to the content that was put forward at the meeting which was very disappointing. It was based on a report by a consultancy firm whose name I will not mention in today's debate, but the content, after reading the report from start to finish, I would actually describe as almost inferior because it completely ignored the fact that these long processing times we have had in the complaint system are rooted in a bottleneck at the Tax Appeal Board and not in a problem with the layman element of our complaint structure. Therefore, it is absolutely crucial to address that the Tax Appeals Board should have the opportunity not to be a bottleneck, but it should not be used as an excuse to destroy the layman element of our tax complaint structure. In addition, there will also be 15 rating boards with a large number of members. This is something that I think we in the Danish Parliament should follow a little bit. After all, it is about how something that is a layman's institution is playing with the fact that there are some people who have tax expertise. It may of course be beneficial in that the tax courts might be able to discuss more with the tax courts the assessments that come from the Tax Appeals Board, but it may also be an undermining of the very layman element of our structure in the area of complaints. But the starting point for Enhedslisten is, of course, that, as we think it is a good deal, we are in favour of this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409145256	Thank you and thank you for the overwhelmingly positive receipt of the bill. It is always good when there is broad political support behind a proposal, because then, almost regardless of what happens, there is also a parliamentary election, which in principle, depending on when it comes, can interrupt the consideration of this bill. This indicates this in the light of the reports that the rapporteurs have given here today. It is a matter for me, as Minister for Taxation, to strengthen legal certainty in the field of taxation. It is crucial that both citizens and companies can be confident that when they meet the tax administration they receive proper treatment. This will be ensured, inter alia, by: We know that there are some rights and that we have reasonable rules. With the first three pieces of legal certainty, together with the parties that have backed them up, the government has, fortunately, made a great deal of progress in strengthening legal certainty in the field of taxation. However, it is important to me and, I hope, also to many taxpayers and companies who are rightly or wrongly caught up in the fact that we are sticking to and focusing on the legal certainty agenda. This is what the government wants to do with this bill, which translates the legislative initiatives in the agreement on a new appeal structure: a faster way through the appeal system, legal certainty package IV, which is also the heading of the bill. Legal certainty has been challenged in the area of complaints as a result of what a number of rapporteurs have also said, namely that it takes far too long to get an answer to your complaint. And just to put it at the forefront: if you as a citizen complain and there goes year after year when you do not get answers, then it may well be that after 3 years or 5 years or perhaps 10 years you get an answer, that you were right, but if you are not here anymore or are in another life situation, you cannot use that answer for much. So legal certainty in the appeal procedure, for my part, is also about the fact that there is a reasonable amount of time, from a complaint to a response to a complaint, and vice versa, that as a citizen or a company you have an obligation to make an informed complaint in the first place, that is to say, things you can do yourself to get faster treatment. But the processing time has been far too long in the Tax Appeals Board. We are then trying to do this with this agreement and this bill, if not up to it, but then help to bring down the processing time and, in particular, for new cases. A number of the measures put into effect in the draft law are, after all, about a more effective process of complaint and about increasing the productivity of the Tax Appeals Authority, while at the same time the tax insight is that it is not just faster decisions, but real decisions that come quickly. This will strengthen the legal certainty of both citizens and companies. The draft law must be seen in conjunction with a new structure for the appeal boards, which, after all, is preserved and future-proofed on a strong layman element with a focus on local anchoring. I would say that with the things we have agreed, we ensure a stronger layman representation than in the past, not only more laymen, but also a stronger layman representation. After all, we are removing what has been very popular in advisory circles, namely that you can choose from, whether you want to have your case dealt with in the Boards of Appeal or in the National Tax Court. There has probably been a tendency for some advisors to think that it was best for their clients to have the matter dealt with by the National Tax Court. There may of course be some professional reasons for this, but to some extent one cannot rule out the fact that there is cost compensation in one place and not the other, also plays a role in the distribution of the cases. Now we say that the smaller and non-principled cases must be settled by the tax courts and the more fundamental cases, and in particular those where external assistance is needed from tax lawyers and accountants or others, must be carried out by the National Tax Court and with the possibility that if a case is a matter of principle and has ended in the wrong place, it can come to the right place on request. So, with those comments, I would like to thank you for the reception that has been given to the proposal. I should like briefly to mention some of the points that have been made by a few of the rapporteurs. The Social Democrats' rapporteur, sir. Jesper Petersen is on the Treasure Appeals Board and on how it works. It is, of course, true that when not enough cases have been handed out to the Tax Appeals Boards, one can point to the Tax Appeals Board as a secretariat and ask if they have done their job well enough. There are certainly some things that could have been done better. Sir. Rune Lund also mentions it and refers to a report made by Deloitte that is not a secret which, according to the framework offer, makes consultant reports in the Treasury area, where there is also a look at how to optimize efficiency without compromising on the professional level. There we have had a board that has not worked optimally. The individual employees cannot be blamed for this, and I do not think that management can be blamed for it either. It's a product of the fact that back in 2013, when you made the latest change in this area, you created a whole new government, but it takes time to build up that governance. Departments of the board had also been placed in some places where it was difficult to recruit employees. It is typically lawyers who are to be recruited to the Tax Appeals Board, and at some of the sites they had, there was an employee turnover of up to 30%. a year. And if you build up a new board, where you have to hire a lot of new employees, typically newly trained employees, a place where you don't have that workforce, and where people have to commute a lot, you'll get into a situation where there's a large turnover of employees, and that, of course, puts itself in productivity. We have learned from this lesson, and that is why we can now value up and down the Tax Appeals Board as to where we can get the necessary manpower, and I think we have a much better management today than we had years ago. And with the changes that we are proposing here, a good regulatory and administrative framework will be created in the form of more money for more employees, ensuring that we can bring the processing time down to a reasonable level. Finally, I would just like to thank you once again for your cooperation with all those who have mentioned it. It has been a long process in connection with this legal certainty package, and there have been ice and other things involved, what Mr. Rune Lund also mentioned, as I was a big word of bribery, trying to make the rapporteurs in a good mood by making sure that they didn't negotiate on an empty stomach. I do not know whether it is because of it or because of the lengthy process, but we have at least ended up with something that I think is a good product and which suggests that all the parliamentary parties can back up. So I, too, would like to sign for that.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409153014	Thank you. The purpose of this bill is to simplify and amend legislation relating to the recovery of debts by the tax administration so that, in the future, a more secure and effective recovery of public debt can take place. The Danish tax system is far too complicated, and that is actually a large part of the reason why it went so wrong with EFI. When you have a very complicated tax and recovery system, it also requires a very, very large IT system to support the legislation. So we must therefore create a simpler system and that is what we are taking a step to do now. It can also be said that this is a small step towards restoring confidence in the tax administration. It is a long process, but we are now taking a step in the right direction. With what we are doing now, we are making the rules simpler in relation to obsolescence, and we are also making the legislation more clear with the aim of making digitalisation more flexible and simpler. This is, for example, the case. on the handling of set-off for transport, legal fees and situations where several debtors are liable for the same claim; all small, sensible measures, which will give us a slightly simpler system so that it will also be a little easier to ensure proper recovery. It is a long journey we are making, but this is a small step in the right direction and we can of course support the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409153146	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments. Then it's the tax minister.

In 2018, the Danish Debt Authority recovered 7.1 billion. kr., and that is the highest figure in recent times. The draft law we are dealing with today strengthens recovery on a number of points. The proposed amendments are aimed at simplifying and system-supporting the obsolescence sorting in the new recovery system. A number of the rapporteurs have also referred to this and the proposal thus provides for further simplifications of the rules on limitation periods. ParlaMint-DK_20190409154248	Thank you for that, and thank you for the opportunity to talk about an important issue here today, namely the whole of our European cooperation. I would very much like to talk about the great advantages of free movement, which I think we should be determined to safeguard. I would also like to talk about the specific challenges associated with free movement in terms of the unacceptable conditions and illegal practices that may occur. That is why the government is also concerned about doing something about it. However, we are concerned about what we can do about it here and now, or at least in the near future. If I were to talk about the benefits of the European Union and the internal market, I would start by saying that this is now the same as kicking in an open door. The Danes are more convinced than ever before. This is probably due in particular to the fact that Britain is currently a test laboratory, it can be said, for how people can see what it will mean to be outside of binding cooperation. And it has become apparent that the European Union is a genuine common market. But it may also mean something that we have a visible Danish Commissioner in the field of competition, which among other things. has given the large tech companies a number of large fines which the individual countries could not have dealt with individually. More facts have therefore come into the debate and we know that more facts are generally good for the political debate. After all, the Danish Parliament has had its own inquiry into this matter. The Danish Parliament thus measured the positions of the participants in the Danish Parliament's meeting six months ago and measured both before and after the meeting. Before the public hearing, 38% thought. of the participants, that Denmark should join the EU without or with fewer reservations than today, and after the popular consultation the number had risen to 59 per cent. So with others, facts are a good thing. Therefore, when we come to the question of a labour market protocol, I would also like to take a look at the facts. I believe that the fact is that the right to free movement and the rights of workers are equal. I think there is a legal basis for saying that, and here I can also refer to the answers that I gave in the Committee on European Affairs last week. So, in order to get a little more clarity on the table, I would also like to start with two examples, which I think illustrate some challenges posed by this motion for a resolution. If, in a public tender for construction tasks, the Region of Flanders requires workers to speak fluently in the interests of safety at work, they will be able to do so by means of a protocol without a proportionality assessment because the protection of workers takes precedence over freedom of movement. Is that how the protocol is to be understood? And if, in an invitation to tender for the digitisation of documents, the City of Dortmund requires that the workers of the suppliers should be subject to German rules of termination, they have previously had a contract with a Danish company and they think that the Danish companies are engaged in unfair competition with their flexible contracts of employment, will they be able to impose such a requirement by means of a protocol? I understand the authors of the motion for a resolution that they believe that what Flanders and Dortmund have received here should be allowed to do. And I believe that in many ways it will put an end to the internal market. But we can think about all sorts of other examples where the Member States can cover themselves in protecting workers' rights, and that will also affect Danish companies and their workers abroad. The practice of the European Court of Justice, as it is known in particular in the area of goods, has meant that Member States are not able to make every possible creative leap to restrict free trade in the internal market. At the same time, the caselaw of the Court of Justice has meant that, where Member States have legitimate grounds, they may adopt their own measures if they are proportionate. Of course, this requires that no directive or regulation has harmonised the specific area; then, as a starting point, an EU country cannot impose higher requirements in the directive or regulation. A directive and a regulation reflect the fact that EU legislation has balanced the various considerations, so that it is, of course, binding. With a protocol, you're gonna put this whole mechanism out of effect. When the government is required to work for a protocol, it is a reasonable requirement to make it clear what the protocol means. In fact, I do not think this proposal is quite clear. I also understand that the proposers want protection against wage dumping, and we do not, according to the Treaty today. The authors of the proposal state that, inter alia: is intended to prevent us from introducing a minimum wage. But that is a lot to ask of the other EU countries. The other EU countries must allow us to introduce a restriction on freedom of movement, because if we tell the other countries that we want to be allowed to introduce rules on equal pay for equal work, then they can say: there you are free to do so. Apart from that, I do not want to change anything fundamental about access to the Danish labour market, so even if the other countries allowed us to do so, I do not think it should be used. Indeed, we benefit greatly from the many workers who come here, and here I would like to refer to the answers I have given. Finally, I would like to say that there are specific challenges that need to be addressed. It cannot wait for Treaty changes that can take years and days. Moreover, I do not think that a change in the Treaty would help very much. Of course, we can regulate so much, but it doesn't help if smart backers can bypass the rules by making structures such as this. mailbox companies and the like. And if we put forward the wish for a treaty change, I think we will open Pandora's box where the other countries may have wishes that will undermine our Danish labour market model. Because we cannot and should not wait for an amendment to the Treaty, it is essential that we take action with enforcement and control, and we must do so at national level, but we must also do so at European level. I would like to continue the dialogue on this in connection with the committee's work, which is now going to take place between the first and second readings, and I would also, of course, like to elaborate on my views on the case law, as I have explained in the recent replies to the European Committee. In essence, I think that this area should be examined in greater depth before this motion for a resolution could be supported, and that also means that the government cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409155833	Thank you for that, President. Basically, we in the Social Democratic Party are very, very much in favour of the EU project. We are also very much in favour of freedom of movement and we are very much in favour of the internal market. But we also believe basically, and I think this is beyond doubt, that with all the gains, all the benefits that have come from both free movement and the internal market there have also been a large number of very large and, unfortunately, still unresolved challenges and problems. The fundamental problem when we look at it is that the four freedoms, in particular the principle of freedom of movement for workers and the free movement of services, have tended not only to be in Denmark, but in a large number of European countries to create a race against the bottom in terms of wages, working conditions and rights in some sectors. We believe that the idea of a social protocol is the right way to go, basically inspired by what the Danish People's Party also refers to in the text, namely the proposal from the European trade union movement which basically equates the principle of the four freedoms and, on the other hand, respect for workers' fundamental rights, working conditions and salaries, etc. The Danish People's Party calls it a labour market protocol in today's motion for a resolution. Part of the reflection in the Social Democratic Party on brexit and a large number of other things was a reflection that we had also hoped for was deeper in the Member States, but it has become little more than a sailing trip on the river in Bratislava and a White Paper from Jean-Claude Juncker has been about there having been no such respect in a large number of countries for the fundamental rights and salaries of the shortest and lowest paid people in particular, and that social dumping has challenged it. We believe that when one day comes it will not be tomorrow, nor will it be next year when Treaty changes are again being discussed, there will, in fact, be a strong Danish view to stand on: that we should of course have an alignment of the Treaties so that these principles are equivalent. We basically mean what was also in the motion for a resolution, which was barely one and a half years ago. After all, it was not adopted on a social protocol. At that time, the Danish People's Party had a challenge with what I think, Mr. Kenneth Kristensen Berth formulated as a standstill clause on social rights. We did not see the challenge, just as we do not see any challenge in the wordings mentioned by the Danish People's Party today. That is why we just want to express our support for this fundamental principle that freedom of movement must also be fair movement. We believe that this is doing the whole EU project and the internal market not only a short-term but also a long-term service, that is to say, to ensure the future of this whole project. We would, of course, like to take this further in the second reading and in discussions on it, and so on, in the light of what the Minister has said today. However, it is very, very important for us that what remains after the examination of this proposal is that we want fundamental equality of treatment between the four freedoms, on the one hand, and fundamental respect for workers' fundamental rights, wages and working conditions, on the other. And there, as the Minister rightly says, we also need to be concerned about what we can do here and now. Because this is something that is intended for one day or another when Treaty changes should be opened up. This is not quite the case, but it should not stop us from having this discussion and from doing different things tomorrow. Tomorrow, for example, contrary to what this government has done, we could increase control in the rest places. We could also continue to push for which I will also sign for us to have done widely in the Danish Parliament that the Posting of Workers Directive is constantly being addressed, so that we have a real policy of having the same pay for the same work in the same place. We can press even harder for the road package to become something and so on. However, all this should not stop us from also having an opinion on where it is that we can do something about the Treaties, so that this EU project, which has helped to create a lot of growth, a lot of jobs and a lot of peace for that matter, is also viable in 25 and 50 years' time and for that matter even further in the future. That is the crux of the matter. And the verdict was that it cannot be accepted by the EU that posted companies should be faced with a requirement that they should comply fully with a Danish or a Swedish or any other agreement. That is what is at the heart of the matter. I have to say that both the Minister and the Left rapporteur are trying to minimize what was actually in that judgment. For there was more and more to it than that it should be transparent for the foreign company, what it really was for the requirements laid down in the agreement. I guess it can't be that complicated. It was written down on a piece of paper, so you could read it. It is true that the Court of Justice said so, but it is of course problematic. If this is problematic for a Swedish agreement, it is also problematic for Danish agreements. But there was more than that. Indeed, it stated that the list of requirements which must be included in the Posting of Workers Directive for foreign companies is an exhaustive list. It was made very, very clear that demands that went beyond those described in the Posting of Workers should not be made. And what does it say? It is that when we talk about wages and conditions of employment, you can only require the minimum wage and the overtime payment period. All the other elements of a Danish agreement, from local wage negotiations to pensions etc., must not be required. Then it is, of course, that the Court of Justice penalises and allows wage-pressing. Then the Court also allowed itself to judge whether the salary described in the Swedish building worker's agreement was a minimum wage. The Court did not consider that to be the case. That is all one needs to know in order to conclude that the European Court of Justice has made a totally unacceptable interference in the negotiating and conflict law of the Danish trade union movement and the entire European trade union movement. If there was no problem, as the rapporteur of the Left and the Minister almost also says, why did it feel obliged in Denmark to make legislation which imposed on Danish trade unions that, when it came to these foreign companies, they were not allowed to demand the full agreement? Then there are many who are trying to create a lot of confusion about what this proposal is about, i.e. that it should mean more EU regulation, and what do I know? This will mean less EU regulation in the area we are talking about here, because it will mean accepting countries' rules of play on how they deal with the whole labour market issue of wage formation, and that is what is at the heart of it. When you are opposed to this, it is because you agree with the basic idea of the EU rules on free movement and that they should be allowed to overrule the attempts of the trade union movement and individual Member States to protect themselves from social dumping. That is why such a social protocol is necessary. It is possible that the aim of the Danish People's Party is to limit the approach of foreign workers to the Danish labour market, but there is nothing about this in the proposal on which it is all based, namely the proposal of the European trade union movement.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409163804	Thank you for the floor. The authors of the motion for a resolution state the purpose of the motion for a resolution here, namely that trade union rights should be equated with the four freedoms of the European Union. After all, this is an interesting question we are addressing here, because the national regulation of the labour market and the four freedoms are something that can be at odds with each other. In southern Jutland, where I live, the transport industry fills a great deal, and it is with great regret that in these years the European Union is moving towards deliberalisation, less freedom of movement for road transport. If you are my age, you can remember bus trips to Italy with hugely long queues at border crossings. They passed trucks that held and had to be inspected. The Brenner Pass was a horror example. You couldn't help but notice that. Then the internal market came, and these cows vanished into almost nothing, and now it's all gotten more troublesome again. Germany has rules on minimum wage and no public IT, so all the information must be sent to a fax in Cologne, and this causes a great deal of trouble for Danish companies. And Danish companies cannot really understand it because the collective wage on which all Danish drivers drive is far, far above the German minimum wage. But no relaxation can be brought in there. Austria has been given national rules which are more stringent than in the past. France has it. And where Italy is going, I don't think anyone really knows. I have an acquaintance who is a trucker, and he says that it is just before he has to have a trailer with documentation when you drive down Europe's truck today. That is why, of course, there must be a fair balance between the rights of workers and the four freedoms, and I think that the balance is reasonable today. Because it is possible to organise itself in such a way as to restrict competition by introducing technical barriers to trade by the back door or by making it possible to introduce them. And I am certainly not in favour of technical barriers to trade, and my party is not in favour either. It must be possible to insist that the work can be carried out under regulated conditions, and that can actually be regulated today. So, in my view and in our view, there is already a balance between trade union rights and the four freedoms, what the proposers say there is not. Will it ever be exactly the way the proposers want it? I think it is a bit in the air that there will come some fantastic day when we in Denmark just get things the way we want them. I don't think you should be too disappointed if that day doesn't come. Clearly, when all EU countries have to agree on a treaty, it is very little likely that there will ever be a treaty that one party considers to be perfect. That is also the case with employees' rights. National systems and traditions differ, the degree of organisation varies in different national labour markets. The balance between legislation and contractual regulation is very different in the different countries. That is why I do not think it can be said that the European Court of Justice is imposing a particular model. It makes some distinctions between different considerations, and it does so in a sensible way. That is why a new treaty will also reflect a whole lot of compromises. It will never be as perfect as the proposers want. So we think that the labour market protocol that is being outlined goes too far and that is why we do not support the resolution. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409170139	Thank you. This is an interesting debate because there is a lot of devil in the detail here. In the Radical Left, I do not think anyone has missed very strong supporters of European cooperation in the European Union. We are also very strongly in favour of the whole foundation on which EU cooperation is based, namely freedom of movement. Slowly and surely, over the many years we have been a member, and also before that, we have agreed the rules of the game for the way we act and act with each other in the European Union. It has been of great benefit to us, as citizens, that we have safe and more orderly conditions when shopping across European borders. For that is what an internal market is all about: it is about regulating it so that it is really free and not anarchy. And the European labour market that has been developed here is a huge achievement. This is an unconditional advantage both for Denmark and for the other EU Member States. Over the years, this has counteracted massive bottleneck problems in Denmark. This has led to a marked improvement in the standard of living in Central and Eastern Europe and in southern Europe. This has made the European Union much more competitive at a global level, and it is only becoming more important and more important. We in the Radical Left are also wholeheartedly involved in the work on the EU's social pillar, which is precisely about creating even better and more equal working conditions, better working conditions and fair competition in the labour market. For the European labour market is not a game of chairs nor is the Danish labour market where one has to push one another away to secure a place to sit. It is perhaps more such a football field where we need to ensure common rules for equal competition on equal terms. We also want, as several rapporteurs have said, to crack down on social dumping. If you work in Denmark, it must be done on proper pay and working conditions, and the same applies in other countries. We believe that Europe should, to a much greater extent, actually be a beacon for the future welfare model. But that is not just my impression; I have been confirmed by the authors of the motion for a resolution: we do not agree on that. The proposers here are concerned about restricting freedom of movement, and we are against that. We are concerned that this proposal, as the Minister has also outlined and illustrated both in response to the European Committee and from here on, will enable individual EU countries to introduce measures specifically for foreign service providers, without the EU institutions having the opportunity to check whether these restrictions are really justified precisely on account of workers, i.e. whether they are proportionate. And it is important for us that we cannot suddenly face arbitrary restrictions and real obstacles when working or establishing ourselves in other parts of the EU's internal market. Because we are opposed to that, too. We are to spend more effort fighting social dumping in Denmark. We are in favour of strengthening the Factory Inspectorate and strongly opposed to the reduction that has been made, but this is a different debate. We would also like to be part of a report that we should have both free and fair competition in the European Union. We would also like to be involved, if possible, in a report containing some of the radical Left's wishes for the future European Union. For example, we want the Commission to have increased powers to sanction Member States that provide unfair tax advantages to large companies. We want the EU's Structural Funds to be used as a sanction against Member States that do not comply with the agreed rules. In other words, we also want a number of workers' rights to be regulated in the EU treaties in the long term. We want European minimum standards for workers' rights. We want pan-European rules for pilots and lorry drivers and others who work transnationally. We want a European children's and education fund to ensure a significant education boost and ensure that Europe's children and young people, indeed all Europeans in this way, get the best start to life. However, we are opposed to combating or restricting free movement. As I said, we do not see the labour market as a game of chairs, and we believe that limiting freedom of movement in the way we read this proposal would be very damaging to Denmark and to the Danish economy and to Danish welfare.

It is absolutely crucial for the SF that we get an agreement at EU level that cannot be amended afterwards by the European Court of Justice. ParlaMint-DK_20190409174858	The debate is open. Minister of Employment.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409174921	With the resolution we are debating today, the Socialist People's Party is proposing two things. Firstly, the daily allowance rate will be increased by 25%. for the first 2 months of unemployment. The increase shall cover persons who have been in employment for at least 4 out of the preceding 5 years. Secondly, there will be a halving of the cash allowance quota for beneficiaries under 25 years of age without training, who will have their daily allowance reduced by half after 26 weeks of unemployment. The Socialist People's Party justifies the proposal by reducing the coverage of benefits, shortening the insurance period and the risk of undermining solidarity in the benefit system. I think it is appropriate to start by stating that we already have a good and secure benefit system. With the agreement on a safer benefit system, a broad majority in the Folketing has improved the benefit system. We have created an unemployment benefit system that provides the unemployed with a financial safety net and a clear incentive to get to work as well. The agreement is based on the overall recommendations of the benefit commission, where the social partners were also represented. The daily allowance commission found that during the last two decades there has been no substantial erosion of the daily allowance and thus the financial security of the benefit system. When we look forward in time, it is true that among other things. the 2012 tax agreement and the annual reduction in the regulation of a wide range of public transfer income would increase the economic gap between employment and transfer income. But in the government we basically think it is sensible that it pays to work. And it makes sense now that it is better to work than to get out of work. That is why it is also agreements which the government wholeheartedly supports and which, moreover, a broad majority in the Danish Parliament supports. I would also like to remind you that the level of compensation depends on how much you earn. That goes without saying. The level of compensation for a person with an income of just under DKK 22,800. is 90%, whereas for a person with a income of just over DKK 51,000. is approximately 40%. The lowest paid who have the least to do with can maintain a benefit close to the previous income. I therefore believe that we have a solid financial safety net under our employees, and when four out of five today are members of an unemployment insurance fund, I believe that this testifies to the fact that our benefit system remains attractive. Danish employees have not turned their backs on the benefit system. In other words, solidarity in the system is still intact. I would also like to mention that in its work, the Commission has looked at the possibilities of providing unemployed people with a strong employment history with a higher rate for a temporary period, i.e. a proposal somewhat similar to that put forward by the SF. The benefit commission pointed out that this would increase the financial coverage of unemployment for the persons covered by the scheme. But it will also have a negative incentive effect. Higher levels of compensation will result in fewer people moving from unemployment to employment, and this will also reduce structural employment. If one chose to implement such a proposal, there would be a need to counteract a decline in structural employment by other measures. This was one of the main reasons why the benefit commission did not propose a temporary higher unemployment benefit rate for unemployed persons with a strong prior employment, and this is also a major reason why the government does not support the SF's proposals. In concrete terms, the proposal will lead to a reduction in employment of some 2,400 full-time people. That is not wise, and it is particularly not wise in the situation we are in at this time. The second part of the resolution is to halve the cash allowance for young people under 25 years of age without training, who get half their rate after 26 weeks of unemployment. The rules on reduction for this specific group of young unemployed people aim to increase the incentive to take training in the ordinary education system rather than stay in the benefit system for several years. I think the SF, with the proposal to reduce the quota for this group of young people, overlooks the fact that it is very bureaucratic and a difficult solution to manage, because in that case the quota must be constantly increased and reduced. In addition, it seems to me that this is an unnecessary proposal in many ways, because already today it is actually a very attractive insurance scheme, even when young people have a reduced daily allowance of DKK 9.433. monthly before tax. An insurance fund membership costs in membership fees to the state just under DKK 4,200. annual. Even including the administrative contribution fixed by the cashier of an average of DKK 1,400. the annual quota does not constitute one month's daily subsistence allowance. In addition, the reduction in the rate does not apply to the entire benefit period but only after 26 weeks of unemployment. So all in all, membership of an insurance fund remains a good business when you are young enough to be unlucky to become available. In conclusion, the government cannot support the proposal here. Firstly, we believe that we already have a safe and sound benefit system which four out of five employees support. Secondly, there must also be funding of approx. 850 million kr. annual. It's not just a grip in your pocket. Thirdly, it will affect employment in a very negative way: the proposal will mean that some 2,400 full-time people will be on unemployment benefit instead of in employment, and it is not a price that the government thinks should be paid. For these reasons, we cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409184515	The debate is open. Minister of Employment.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409184522	With the resolution we are debating today, the Enhedslisten will provide everyone in the cash aid system with a twelve-month clarification guarantee. According to the proposal, the declaration guarantee means that within 12 months, and in exceptional cases not more than 24 months, municipalities must have established whether citizens should be visited for ordinary employment, training, rehabilitation, resource development, flexible jobs or early retirement. As I see it, the proposal is basically about how we prevent the recipients of cash benefits from being stuck in the system and that they will also be helped as quickly as possible. This is a sympathetic agenda which I myself and the government are deeply concerned with, and which we have also used countless meetings and consultations to discuss. In many ways, I fully agree with Enhedslisten that this is a major failure to the many people who have been parked on cash benefits year after year. Of course, we also owe it to them to make an effort there. But I would also like to start by saying that I do not believe that this so-called clarification guarantee is the solution. We run the risk that it will be another procedural requirement that locks the efforts of the municipalities for the citizens, and we are basically in danger of throwing good money at something that is not going to make a real difference. I would like to start by explaining what is at all in the fact that the municipalities have to clarify the citizens, because I think that is important for us to get this right. First of all, I would like to remind you that when we talk about long-term cash aid recipients, we are talking about a group of citizens who typically have problems other than unemployment. Some citizens have very complex problems, and it is also not the case that the situation of the citizen is static. It can change, and it can change over time. When a citizen comes to the municipality for the first time and applies for cash benefits, a decision is quickly made as to whether the citizen is entitled to support. It is, of course, absolutely crucial that the municipality takes a position quickly, so that the citizen has a dependency base. In the case of citizens who have problems other than unemployment, it is also up to the municipality to decide what efforts should be made. This is done on the basis of an individual assessment of the citizens' specific challenges. There is also a requirement for municipalities to carry out continuous follow-up in the cases precisely to ensure that citizens are not just left to themselves. For citizens with a permanent and significant reduction in their ability to work, the municipality must always assess whether citizens are in the target group for resource progression, flex jobs or early retirement. The municipality will have to carry out a comprehensive social assessment in which it will have to take into account all the opportunities available to provide assistance. It is the municipality's task to ensure that a case is sufficiently clarified and documented before a decision on e.g. a sequence of resources, flexible jobs or early retirement. It will therefore be individual when the municipality has sufficient documentation to make this decision. Unfortunately, when it comes to very complex cases, it may take time to make a social and holistic assessment and thus have the full documentation to make this decision. That is why the good question is this: what will a clarification guarantee actually be able to change? For what should the municipality do when it has to decide after 12 months or after 24 months if information is still lacking in the citizen's case? For example, the municipality is missing. health information in order to assess whether the citizen should be provided with resources, then the municipality cannot make a decision. The municipality cannot take a decision on the basis of the available documentation if there is a lack of information in the case. Therefore, there will be a real risk that, in all cases, a decision will not be taken, even if a clarification guarantee is introduced, for example. 12 months. In these cases, a clarification guarantee would simply lead to an empty decision, which means that the citizen must continue to receive cash benefits. In this context, the introduction of a procedural requirement may in fact lead to a longer procedure, with a further decision to be taken at a time when the case is not clear, while at the same time citizens have a right of appeal. Another reason why I am not in favour of a clarification guarantee is that it is another new procedural requirement. After all, it is not so many days since we last stood here and discussed the LAB law, namely the new Act on active employment efforts, which must, of course, help to simplify the procedural requirements and give the municipalities greater freedom. I am still very much in favour of this, although it was a few days ago that we had the first reading. Therefore, my basic view is that new rigid procedural requirements for the work of municipalities with citizens are not necessarily what needs to be done. I believe that what is needed is for us to hold municipalities more responsible for what is happening. If there are municipalities that fail to meet their responsibilities, they can be placed under administration in the extreme. That is why I have also made it clear that I will not accept that citizens are left to themselves. That is precisely where we have to take advantage of the agreement we have concluded with each other, and then the hammer, if you will say so, has to fall. It's time to follow up. It is precisely the new thing in the LAB agreement that we have made that there is no free ticket to the municipalities to leave the citizens behind, and in the event that this happens, there must be political attention to it. I also think we should welcome the fact that we have launched a number of different initiatives on the basis of the Danish People's Party's wishes. After all, it is the projects that more people have to do with 1 and more people have to do with 2 and that are essentially based on making an extraordinary effort for people in the cash benefits system. "More people are going to have to make a special effort for those who have been in the cash benefits system for more than 5 years, and we have to say that by the end of 2018 we are beginning to see some really good results. The cases have been examined in the vast majority of the municipalities that have been involved, and we can also see that by the end of December 2018, approx. 70% of those who do not belong in the cash benefits system, transferred to other benefits. This applies mainly to unemployment benefit, resource progression and early retirement. I think that's a good number. Of course, it's not 100%, but it's 70%. and, unfortunately, we are also witnessing a political difficulty: there are people who have been parked on cash aid this year and some even for several years, without that being what they should have been parked on. They should have been helped, they should have been on another service. We need to do something about that. That's also why we've had the initiative... more people are going with 1 and now the initiative... more people are going with 2... where 167 million have been set aside. kr. to disseminate good experience, precisely so that more vulnerable citizens in the cash assistance system can get an effort. And it is they who have been there between 1 and 5 years who are involved in more than 2 years. The intention is to ensure that they can get started quickly and ensure the right help and effort so that the individual citizen can move on with his life. This also requires a special degree of political attention, because it is of course also important to make it clear that this is the responsibility of the municipalities. We have then chosen, in exceptional circumstances, to allocate funds to examine the cases, and we have seen there that there has been an enormous local interest in participating in these projects. ParlaMint-DK_20190409194904	We are opening the debate, and we are opening the debate with the Minister of Justice. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409194923	Thank you. The motion for a resolution requires the government to put forward a bill amending the Data Protection Act which allows private individuals to publish photos and videos from private surveillance cameras in connection with forced entry or theft. According to the proposers, only the photos and videos attached to the police report may be made public. I would like to begin by saying that crime cannot be accepted and that it is never fair to be exposed to crime either. This applies to all types of crime and, of course, to burglary and theft. That's exactly why I launched the show last November... security for shopkeepers. The aim of the action is to take tough action against shoplifting with seven concrete initiatives, so that traders have good framework conditions for running the store. This means, among other things, that areas particularly affected by shoplifting must have targeted and strengthened police action and that we must strengthen information for shops so that they know where and how surveillance must be set up. In addition, we strongly oppose foreign thieves who come to Denmark to commit illegal acts. At the same time, here in the Folketing last year, we adopted an amendment to the law on television surveillance which allows operators to share surveillance images under a number of conditions in an internal IT system. In addition, in December 2017, we in the Danish Parliament have tightened the penalty for burglary. We did so by inserting a separate provision on burglary into the criminal code, after which burglary is punished by up to 11⁄2 years of imprisonment, and the penalty can go to prison for up to 6 years if the burglary is of a particularly serious nature. It is extremely insulting to be subjected to burglary or theft, but we cannot, therefore, organise ourselves to share surveillance images of suspected perpetrators on social media, regardless of whether they are possible perpetrators of a crime committed against oneself. Monitoring images on social media are shared to the general public. We therefore need to make sure that the people on display in the public sector are in fact some who are supposed to be perpetrators, so that we do not bring other random people into such a context. It is also important for me to emphasise that it is the police who have the expertise to assess the necessary steps to be taken in the investigation of burglary and theft cases. You can, for example. Imagine a burglary in a library committed by three perpetrators seen on the surveillance from the scene. Surveillance is shared within the police to identify the three men. A police officer recognizes one of the three and through previous cases there are possible associates. The police are thus able to arrest them at the same time so that they cannot agree with their explanations. And in addition, the police have the opportunity to search after e.g. the stolen costs and the clothes they were wearing at the time of the crime. If the surveillance had been published immediately or shortly after the break-in, the perpetrators or friends would probably have seen the pictures, and this had provided an opportunity to reconcile explanations as well as dispose of clothes and costs and do all sorts of other things that would make it difficult for the police to solve the crime. Surveillance images often cannot stand alone in proving that a criminal offence has been committed, and so there is a risk that the perpetrators will be spared punishment if they have been warned through the publication of surveillance. It is therefore important that we let the police do their job. That is also why we have put in place a system whereby the police can publish surveillance images collected by citizens who have been exposed to crime and on whom the police want information. Citizens can then • and it is important to say • share links to the police website on social media or by other means. With this arrangement, it is thus the police who decide whether the images are shared or not. Of course, such a division from the police must take place as soon as possible, and I would like to stress this today: of course this must be done as soon as possible. In this way, we balance the consideration of being able, on the one hand, to help prevent burglary and theft and, on the other hand, to ensure that innocent people are not displayed on public websites and that we do not destroy police investigations into cases. I think that is the right balance. I know the proposers' point of view in this area... it does not come as a shock... but now I have explained why the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409200231	Thank you. The rule in the Code of Procedure today concerning what we are talking about here is Paragraph 818 and it provides that the police can publish their many pictures, etc. by a suspected person, if it is essential for the investigation, in the case of a serious offence which may result in more than one and a half years in prison. Basically, it's the police's decision. It is also the police's decision whether this is a disproportionate offence, that is to say that you do not publish a picture of a 15-year-old who has stolen a Yankie Bar or something like that; it is a decision that today lies with the police. In this respect, it is worth mentioning that the resolution we are dealing with today is about publishing photos and videos from surveillance cameras, that is. from shops and the like. Because we understand the proposal, I would say that the counter piece must be that you can file your material with the police and that the police then review the material. In my optics it could be within 24 hours of any publication. It must of course be in situations where it is quite clear who it is, that there is a certain amount of sharpness, if I may use that expression, so that there can be no confusion; where you can also see that something criminal is happening; where you have no doubt that someone above the age of criminals is doing it, that is. a person who can be punished in the end. In those situations, I think it would be quite fine for the police to be able to publish this and think that they should and should publish these pictures very quickly. ParlaMint-DK_20190409203112	Thank you. Therefore, in June 2017, a broad majority of the Folketing tightened the penalties for insecure begging to ensure the safety of citizens on pedestrian streets, stations, supermarkets and public transport. When we look at the situation today, it can be said that the punishments have worked. Today, citizens experience much less insecurity-building begging in their daily lives than before the punishments came into force in June 2017. According to the Copenhagen Police Department, the punishment has had a clear effect. From 2017 to 2018, there has been a gross decrease in both the number of alerts and charges for begging and charges for insecure begging. In addition, the Attorney General has stated that 70 people have been sentenced to unconditional prison sentences since the punishment was introduced in June 2017 and until the end of February of this year. The positive trend seems to continue, although there is still insecurity-building begging, for example. in the S-traines in the Copenhagen area. It is therefore also my clear assessment that lifting the penalty would have a negative effect on the developments we are experiencing at the moment. I therefore believe that it can be said that the punishment has had a clear effect. The positive developments are not something the government wants to put at risk, and the government cannot therefore support the resolution. The police and the judicial system must continue to be able to tackle insecurity-building begging if the problem is to grow again. That is necessary, and it is reasonable. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409210721	Thank you. The alternative here proposes to roll back the amendment of the law to tighten up the penalty for begging. We do not understand why begging is particularly insecure in pedestrian streets and stations, whereas one obviously does not feel insecure when begging takes place in front of, for example. Christiansborg. We also see that the proposal is contrary to the UN guiding principles of extreme poverty and human rights, which state that states are called upon to abolish the criminalisation of the life-sustaining acts of homeless people carried out in public spaces, such as sleeping, begging, eating, and activities aimed at personal hygiene. At the same time, the prison sentence for begging is extremely expensive for society. The government itself mentions that it costs 8 million. kr. a year, and I wonder if that money could be used wiser? The law that the Alternative will roll back is a legislative amendment from 2017, a tightening of the penalty for begging in selected places, the so-called beggar law. The purpose of the amendment was to combat insecure begging defined as begging in those places, while the background was apparently to scare foreign homeless people from going to Denmark and begging. Since then, it has been shown that ordinary homeless people begging without creating insecurity are being affected by legislation, contrary to what the parties behind the ban promised in 2017. With the closing debate in 2017, the Prime Minister presented that this was a proposal that should not hit all homeless people who asked for a score, but that the proposal should strike down so-called insecurity-building begging. After that, the Social Democratic Party, with a number of others, indicated their willingness to support the proposal. However, it was soon clear that the proposal hits all homeless people. In the alternative, we did not vote in favour of the proposal then, and now we are trying to roll the law back. It has turned out that the proposal punishes all homeless people, and it is against this background illegal for homeless people to ask for a fiver for a cup of coffee. We do not believe that homeless and vulnerable citizens should be punished for acts that result from the existence of social problems, nor do we believe that social problems should be tackled fundamentally by punishing and criminalising socially vulnerable citizens. The question is also, in fact, how big a problem has been raised here. In 2018, the Council of Social Vulnerables presented a study carried out in 2017 which examined the Danes' attitudes to socially vulnerable people. Overall, the study shows that two out of three Danes in question are saddened by society's inadequate help when they see a homeless person on the street. I mean, two out of three are sad about the help we give to the homeless. Every third feels guilty in the same situation, while just 14 percent. They get insecure when they see a homeless person on the street or in a public place. This legislation satisfies the needs of 14 per cent. by the Danes, and how much other legislation does it do? But that does not mean, of course, that there can be no examples of insecure situations, but it does say a little about the fact that this is not a problem that has been as extensive as it is being run up to. Overall, this proposal is therefore both expensive and contrary to the guiding principles of the United Nations, but above all it is a huge attack on the very poorest people in our society, and we find that in the alternative completely unworthy. What we are talking about is typically harmless acts where a homeless person sticks out the cup or a hand and asks for a score for food, coffee or medicine. No one has been harmed since then, and so, of course, it should not be criminal. It is not harassment, insecurity or threatening behaviour, so it is symbolic politics. And if it should happen that someone cannot behave properly, then of course it must have consequences. We have, among other things. the police to ensure, and this is generally the case with harassment and inappropriate behaviour. ParlaMint-DK_20190409211559	Thank you. The motion for a resolution put forward by the Alternative is that the government in this Assembly should take the initiative to abolish the rules on zone bans and, as the proposers put it, stop criminalising the homeless in Denmark. Firstly, citizens throughout Denmark must be able to feel safe, and therefore we cannot and should not accept if people establish themselves and stay in camps that create insecurity in the neighbourhood. Secondly, the rules on zonal bans are not aimed at criminalising the homeless. On the other hand, the rules on zone bans are closely linked to the ban on insecure camps. The rules on insecure camps and zone bans were introduced after Copenhagen in the summers 2016 and 2017 was subjected to a massive influx of homeless people from other countries. It marked the entire city picture and created insecurity. That is the reason for the rules we have today. In the spring of 2017, it was prohibited to establish and reside in camps suitable for creating insecurity in the vicinity, and in spring 2018 it was possible for the police to notify zone bans to an entire municipality to persons who have violated the camp ban. And it's worked. We had a problem, and we solved it. In view of the reports we have received from the police, it has to be said that the rules have been effective. We see far fewer examples of camps in the city picture. I believe that the rules on zone bans have helped to deter people from creating insecurity in Copenhagen. There has subsequently been a lot of debate about the fact that the rules are affecting the homeless. To this I would just like to say that this is not a criminalisation of homelessness, but a criminalisation of camps that create insecurity. With the rules there is a derived effect on the homeless, but it is not by abolishing the rules that we have to solve that problem. Then we would probably be able to look forward to another summer with visiting foreigners and insecure camps in the city picture.
