On the whole, it seems that we can learn a great deal from our neighbouring countries, because there came I think it was last year that a study of what it looks like in the Nordic countries in relation to tackling homelessness, and Denmark was absolutely at the bottom of doing so. We are the country where homelessness has increased most, especially among young people, and I wonder if it should also have a connection with the policy of the present majority of the people, and indeed of the past of low youth benefits, in relation to what young people get to live on. I understand that some of them are here, but the Enhedslisten must also recognise that there is something wrong in coming up here and setting up camps when you come from Eastern Europe. That is a failure on the part of those countries.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409214914	Thank you very much. However, if you could just make sure that no one lives on the streets of Denmark with a snap and a quick or slow change of law, it would be nice, but unfortunately the world is not that simple. There have been examples of this, and I just think we should be honest about it, that is to say that it has been the case. But it is not the law of zoning that creates homelessness. It is not that I recognise that it affects the homeless in a way that was not intended. It's just to get it straight. That is why it is not the case that the removal of the zone ban will eliminate homelessness. The Zone Ban was introduced in a situation where, as many others have said before me, we had a situation of camps of beggars and homeless people that created insecurity. This is what is being done to get rid of it, and it hits so unintentionally. This is a dilemma, and it is a political dilemma, because, on the one hand, we do not want it to happen unintentionally, but, on the other hand, we also have a responsibility to create security for the citizens and to do something about the camps that arose at the time. Sometimes politics is not simple, but actually difficult, and I actually think it is here. I think that we in the Danish Parliament broadly agree on this, at least in the debate it sounds like that efforts in relation to the homeless could be better in many places, and I think this has to do with many different policy areas and interventions in many places. As is the case with the draft law that we dealt with before this, that is to say, the one about punishment for begging, there is also a sunset clause in this zoning bill, which means that, unless Parliament decides otherwise, the law will expire per year. 1 March 2021, i.e. In a little less than two years. And by then I think it's a good idea to take it up in its entirety, including this that it hits unintentionally... I think it's actually gonna be a good idea for all of us to admit it does and see if you can solve it in another way. Sometimes politics is full of nuances and dilemmas, and I think that is actually the case here. But we cannot support this resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20190409215522	Thank you to Mrs Christina Egelund. Now we have come to the rapporteur for the proposers, Mr President. Torsten Gejl from the Alternative. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409215543	Thank you. No, at least we shouldn't give up. We must create a much, much stronger homeless effort. However, the proposal here is to abolish the zone ban, and I hope the President forgives me for saying that the Radicals also support this proposal, just as they supported the last proposal. The zone ban was introduced to limit so-called insecurity-building camps. In particular, the government has done a great deal to reduce the number of homeless foreigners. The vast majority of cases we hear, however, show that the ban often affects ordinary homeless people, both Danish and foreign, who sleep alone on the streets or in pairs. In fact, this means that a ban on homelessness has been put in place in practice. The fact that the zone ban hits Danish homeless is documented by the fact that a large group of Danish homeless people have spent the night in front of Christiansborg here tonight in what they call a safe camp. The night was a demonstration against the zone ban. To understand the situation of the homeless, I slept with them myself last night at the Palace Square. Outwardly it was a very, very peaceful camp. I do not think that any of the citizens who came by on a regular Monday night felt insecure. Nor did people who came by in a late night hour feel uncomfortable about it. Nor was any of the passers-by trying to do anything to the homeless, but it may also be a bit incomprehensible when many people are lying together. On the other hand, I learned that the homeless not only seek together at night to create security and security, but also to warm each other and to help each other. Homeless people often face social challenges and physical and mental illnesses. There were several times last night when a homeless person woke up unhappy or with very severe pain and was helped by the others. Many homeless people are unhappy people, sick people, and the night can be long. It actually touched me last night how more homeless people dropped their own night's sleep to help others in need. I think it gives further reason to let homeless people stay overnight in small groups. I think it's heartless to keep them from sleeping together. If this proposal does not go through, which unfortunately does not suggest it does, then I think we should look again at the proposal from the homeless's own organisation, SAND, where accommodation zones are proposed. They don't want to be on Strøget. They say that you might be able to create some accommodation zones available by metro or train, and then you could say that it is a little less who sleep there together, so that we do not experience large groups of homeless people sleeping together. Furthermore, as the proposer, we also point out that our proposal here is in line with the UN guiding principles of extreme poverty and human rights, which state that states are being called upon to abolish the criminalisation of the life-sustaining acts of homeless people carried out in public spaces, such as sleeping, begging, eating, and activities related to personal hygiene. We find it pointless and unreasonable that homeless people should be criminalised and, in some cases, deported by the municipality simply because they are homeless and are staying with other homeless people. I've been talking to a lot of homeless people in the last few days who've been hitting, kicking and peeing. Some have experienced rape in situations where they lie alone. I think it is heartless that we do not find a solution to leave these people together for a few. Instead of punishing and criminalising homeless people, we recommend that the government strengthen efforts to reduce homelessness in Denmark, increase investment in shelters and cheaper housing. But we also propose that we roll back the zone ban. And as I hope the President will again forgive, I would like to say that with this bill, as with the last one, I thank Enhedslisten, SF and Radicals for their support. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-DK_20190409220033	Thank you. There's nothing to forgive here. Everything that has been said is fully within the framework of what has to be said from the platform.
